Gilmore Girls' Milo Ventimiglia Gives Rare Nod to Ex Alexis Bledel

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Gilmore Girls alum Milo Ventimiglia made a rare reference to his former girlfriend Alexis Bledel, whom he dated while filming the Amy Sherman-Palladino series.

Milo Ventimiglia has been out on the road for a long time.

But when he thinks back to his time on Gilmore Girls and his relationship with costar Alexis Bledel, he feels anything but lonely and so cold.

“What were those days like on set?” he reflected to Us Weekly in an interview published Nov. 2. “Hanging out with Jared Padalecki, being with Alexis—the experience is something that I always lean into, that really fills me up.”

The This Is Us star and Bledel’s onscreen chemistry as Jess and Rory found legs off camera, with the pair quietly dating for almost four years in the aughts before their 2006 breakup. But while their romance wasn’t destined to last, the pair’s relationship was and has always remained amicable.

In fact, some of the memories that stand out clearest for Ventimiglia—who shares two kids with wife Jarah Mariano—involve his former girlfriend.

“I remember shooting on the back lot of Warner Bros. with Alexis and getting on the bus and carrying a bag that the prop guy wanted to make really light,” he said. “But I [said], ‘Yeah, but it looks full and it’s huge, so it should be heavy. Put a sandbag in it.’ Those are the things that I think about.”

But while the Gilmore Girls fandom loves to examine the minutiae of every line, scene and episode—including the famously emphatic way Jesse asked Rory, “Why did you drop out of Yale?”—it’s the more general memories that fill him up like a cup of coffee.

“The episodes are for an audience to pick apart, dissect,” he added. “For us to make it, [it’s] the experience of making it.”

And loving it the way he do, Ventimiglia is ready to go to the ends of the earth to recall every beautiful moment for the upcoming HBO Max documentary about the Amy Sherman-Palladino show.

“I think the world of Amy, and Lauren [Graham],” he said, adding of the doc, “I have no idea what we’re going to talk about. It was 26 years ago that I made the show.”

He added, “It’s funny: I’ll have people run up to me and be like, ‘Hey, do you remember that time on Gilmore when you said this?’ I’m like, ‘That was 26 years ago.’ I’m like, ‘Tell me something about your life from 26 years ago.’ They’re like, ‘I wasn’t born.’”

But even more than a quarter century later, Jess and Rory remain a beloved onscreen pairing and a strong contender in the Gilmore Girls ship wars. For a look back at the show’s other onscreen couples in E!'s official ranking, read on.

He sued her father. He almost destroyed Luke and Lorelai's burgeoning relationship. He went by the nickname Digger. What more do we need to say?

They got married impulsively as teenagers (what a fun trope, said no one ever!) and then he promptly cheated on her. The only thing she got out of this marriage was disappointment and a mean meatloaf recipe.

We still can't believe a proposal with a thousand yellow daisies was wasted on a dud like Max Medin-ugh.

Remember that time Luke randomly married Nicole on a cruise ship? And then she cheated on him? Yeah, we try to forget about it, too.

Sorry, but the fact that he called her Mary (you know, Virgin Mary?) still creeps us out.

Rachel was Luke's first love, whose dreams were just too big for Stars Hollow. She was super-sweet and pushed Luke to realize his feelings for Lorelai. Thanks, girl!

OK, hear us out: Lane deserved more than Zack, who was a convenient filler for the void left by Adam Brody's exit. Sorry, not sorry. He's the rebound, NOT THE ONE.

Two weirdos, one weird relationship. Not the most epic of romances, but she did deal with his night terrors. So, true love.

Dave was definitely the one that got away (or, you know, ran off to become Seth Cohen on The O.C.). Come on, he inscribed his bible, with "This bible belongs to God but is being borrowed by Dave Rygalski." Swoon City, population: us.

You never forget your first love...but that doesn't mean you should continue an on-and-off relationship for over a decade. While we liked Christopher, we didn't love these two together, especially when they thought it was OK to run off and get married without Rory (their daughter!) being there. Unforgivable.

Fact: These two could take over and run their own country. They were made for each other.

Sigh, the unsung heroes of Stars Hollow also made up its most solid couple that flew so under the radar you often forgot about them...until you needed a cup of sugar from next door.

Yes, he made her a car and was her first love. But he also cheated on his wife with Rory. Talk about a great way to lose your virginity. Basically, Dean was ruined for us. Still, their quick reunion during A Year in the Life was random, nostalgic and bittersweet in only the way a catch-up session with your childhood sweetheart can be.

She made him a better person, and he helped her learn what she really wanted to do with her life. Often over-shadowed by Rory's two other major love interests, Logan is the dark horse among Gilmore's guys...and possibly Rory's (spoiler alert!) baby daddy?! We're going to need another revival STAT.

Of all Rory's love interests, he was the one that challenged (and frustrated) her the most. The classic bad boy. If only they had met later in life...and watching their brief interaction during the Netflix revival just proved how potent their chemistry still is.

While Lorelai and Rory's love lives were unpredictable and (deliciously) wrought, there was something so sweet and simple about Sookie and Jackson's love story. You always argue over produce with the one you love!

"Will you just stand still?" Fact: These two had the best and most-earned first kiss in the history of first kisses. And everything else was pretty epic, too. (Let's just all pretend April Nardini, Luke's daughter, never showed up and ruined everything, mmmkay?)

GTA 6 Could Save You $5,000 According To Self-Made Millionaire

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

GTA 6 is the first new game since Mario Kart World to have an $80 standard edition, with a $100 ultimate edition if you want access to all of its in-game stores. That’s a lot of money for just one game, except a self-made millionaire has explained how the one-off, $100 expenditure could end up saving you thousands in the long run.

The millionaire in question is Mark Tilbury, a British millionaire who, among other things, uses social media to share investment and money-saving tips. His latest video has quickly gone viral as it explains why buying a $100 copy of GTA 6 could end up saving you $5,000.

Tilbury begins by breaking down how much entertainment you’re getting per hour in GTA 6, based on the reveal by Rockstar that its story will take 80 hours to play through. Dollars-per-hour isn’t a metric I typically like to consider when weighing up the value of a game, but for the sake of this fun exercise, let’s hear Tilbury out.

If you were to play GTA 6 for 80 hours and then never touch it again, you’d have paid $1.25 for the experience. That’s not bad at all. This is where Tilbury loses me a little, though. He then notes that once you add in side content and map exploration, the price-per-hour for GTA 6 drops to just 50 cents. That’s 200 hours, and while I’m sure many, if not most of us will spend that much time in Leonida, if not more, he doesn’t explain where that 200-hour figure comes from.

Regardless of how much you end up paying per hour for GTA 6, Tilbury still hasn’t explained how buying it for $100 could end up saving you $5,000. The start of the road to that number begins with the assumption that GTA 6 owners will play the game in four hour chunks. Divide that 200-hour total by four, and you get 50 play sessions.

That means, at least according to Tilbury, that your GTA 6 playthrough will result in you opting to not go out and paint the town red at least 50 times. The millionaire hypothesizes that you might spend $100 on any given night out, multiply that number by the 50 nights out you’re not having because you’re at home running around Vice City, and that’s how he concludes that a $100 copy of GTA 6 could end up saving you a cool 5k.

There are a lot of assumptions being made here. Assumptions that not only won’t apply to everyone playing GTA 6, but probably won’t even apply to the average GTA 6 player. From the hours spent with the game to how long they’re playing it each time. Most of all, though, the idea that someone is choosing to stay in and play GTA 6 over going out and spending $100 each time not once, not twice, but 50 times - that’s just not happening. The big takeaway here, again, as much as I don’t like to boil a game down to dollars spent vs. hours played, is that for $80, or $100, you are almost certainly going to get your money’s worth out of GTA 6.

A dark detective thriller where folklore is real, violence is chilling, and every choice has consequences.

Bill Hader To Guest Star On ‘Abbott Elementary’ This Season

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Bill Hader will make a guest appearance on ABC’s Abbott Elementary this season, though no word yet on character, plot or other details.

Hader made the announcement during his appearance on Jimmy Kimmel Live! last night but gave away no details. “I’m excited,” he deadpanned, leaving us to guess the rest.

The guest appearance makes sense given that Hader and Abbott creator and actor Quinta Brunson recently teamed up for the upcoming animated film The Cat in the Hat, providing the voices of, respectively, The Cat and a new character named Sherri. Warner Bros. Animation’s The Cat in the Hat opens in theaters on Nov. 6. Watch on Deadline

Hader was most seen in Larry David’s Life, Larry and the Pursuit of Unhappiness – he played Abe Lincoln – and even more recently appears as himself in the current Netflix documentary SCTV: The Greatest Show You’ve Never Heard Of, a film exec produced by Kimmel that traces the history of the groundbreaking and beloved comedy sketch show.

Hader, though a lifelong fan of SCTV, is best known for the eight seasons he spent on rival Saturday Night Live from 2005 to 2013. He also created and starred in the HBO series Barry from 2019 to 2023, earning two Emmy Awards.

Jason Statham and Guy Ritchie Officially Reunite for New Netflix Movie

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

When two of the most consistent and outstanding action star-director combos in Hollywood get to history, we like to sit up and pay attention. Jason Statham and Guy Ritchie are officially reuniting for Dogfight, a brand-new Netflix action thriller written, directed, and produced by Ritchie, with Statham set to star. According to Deadline, the movie is described as a stylish revenge thriller set within London's criminal underworld, with production scheduled to begin in the United Kingdom in early 2027.

This one will be the seventh collaboration between the two British action heavyweights, who first worked together on Ritchie's 1998 directorial debut, Lock, Stock and Two Smoking Barrels. While Netflix has yet to reveal specific plot details or additional cast members, the combination of Statham, Ritchie, and London's criminal underworld should give audiences a fairly good idea of the sort of movie they're getting. We'll get plenty of violence, we'll get plenty of questionable characters, and we'll get one idiot who makes the dumb decision to upset Jason Statham.

Before Dogfight arrives, the pair will reunite for Viva La Madness, an adaptation of J.J. Connolly's novel of the same name. The movie serves as a sequel to Connolly's Layer Cake, which was previously adapted into the acclaimed 2004 crime thriller starring Daniel Craig.

Ritchie and Connolly co-wrote the screenplay for Viva La Madness, which will feature an ensemble including Statham, Vinnie Jones (Snatch), Jason Isaacs (The White Lotus), Babs Olusanmokun (Dune), Camila Mendes (Riverdale), Ben Foster (Hell or High Water), Jonny Lee Miller (Elementary), and Raúl Alejandro, who will make his feature film debut.

The new Netflix project follows the pair's previous collaborations on Lock, Stock and Two Smoking Barrels, Snatch, Revolver, Wrath of Man, and Operation Fortune: Ruse de Guerre, with Viva La Madness set to become their sixth movie together.

The announcement also continues Ritchie's increasingly busy relationship with Netflix, which has become home to several of his recent projects, including The Gentlemen. And with Statham continuing to dominate the action genre, another collaboration between the two seems like a fairly safe bet for anybody who enjoys watching criminals make terrible decisions.

Production on Dogfight is scheduled to begin in early 2027 in the United Kingdom. Netflix has not yet announced an official release date.

7 Famous James Bond Auditions That Almost Changed 007 Forever

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Given the popularity of the suave super spy and his adventures, it's par for the course that many talented actors have gone for and just missed out on playing James Bond - with some even making it to the audition stage. With major legacy roles like James Bond or Batman, the matter of casting is usually an intensive-sounding one, with those involved in picking these iconic heroes generally wanting to test the waters with a fair range of actors to make sure the right one is selected.

As specific visions for movies are often crafted before casting begins, plenty of actors who could logically nail specific characters can miss out on parts like that of 007 because they don't fit the exact parameters required, rather than not being good enough at their craft. This also means plenty of actors can audition for the role and not get it even if they seemed like solid picks for the opportunity, because the nuance of what's being looked for may contrast with said actor's understanding of what's being asked for.

While this is a shame for those who narrowly missed out on the chance to play James Bond, it does make learning the behind the scenes stories about who almost took on specific eras of 007's story endlessly more engrossing. This is doubly true given that all of these actors would've brought about a different overall landscape for the world of James Bond movies in their own ways.

Years before he would become known as the DCEU's Superman - and later Geralt of Rivia in Netflix's The Witcher - Henry Cavill auditioned for the role of James Bond, hoping to be the actor picked for the era of 007 that would start with Casino Royale.

Though Casino Royale's James Bond role would go to Daniel Craig instead, Cavill's audition has been noted to be a promising one. Director Martin Campbell stated that Cavill's performance was solid, and that he was passed on for the role more because he was slightly too young for the version of Bond that was envisioned (as per The Hollywood Reporter), rather than this seeming to be a matter of acting talent.

Had Cavill been cast for the role, this would have changed two major franchises in a big way. Not only would the 007 series have gotten a Bond whose physicality and history with action stories would have no doubt been reflected in his on-screen performance, the DCEU also would seemingly have had to cast a different Superman too, to avoid filming conflicts.

Sam Neill's audition for 007 came after Roger Moore's time as Bond came to a conclusion, with Neill being one of the viable candidates for his replacement in The Living Daylights. Interestingly enough, it seems as though Neill was very much a frontrunner for the film, with director John Glen reportedly having been inclined towards picking the actor for the role (as per The Hollywood Reporter), though Timothy Dalton would be cast instead.

Given Neill was and is still best known for his role as Dr. Alan Grant in the Jurassic Park series, it would have been interesting to see whether broader audiences struggled more to see him as the kind of more ruthless and no-holds-barred killer that we saw with Dalton's Bond, though Neill certainly did have a look that would have meshed well with the usual debonair getup of the secret agent.

Indeed, while Neill's acting talents suggest he would have been more than capable in this kind of role, it also bears to reason his casting could have influenced the film to divert from the path it took with Timothy Dalton's own Bond to begin with. Given how this set up the path to the modern Bond films we have today, it's not impossible that this move alone could have totally transformed the James Bond timeline from the 80s up until today.

The Bond series has flirted on and off with the idea of an American actor playing the role for a long time now. James Brolin was one of the earlier efforts to consider an American actor for the role, following then-current James Bond actor Roger Moore considering retiring after 1981's For Your Eyes Only.

Brolin's screen test for the role displayed his ability to do some convincing secret spy fight scenes, and it seemed for a short while as though he was set to be the next Bond. However, these plans failed to come to fruition due to Moore returning to the role for 1983's Octopussy.

With that said, it's fascinating to consider what might have been the case if Brolin had indeed gotten the role. Brolin's capacity for comedy, and his decidedly varied filmography in terms of genre means he could have well been the most all-rounded Bond casting to date had things become reality. Similarly, the actor's history with sci-fi before the 1980s might have lent itself nicely to a 007 who was very much at home with using larger-than-life spy gadgets in a casual and cool manner.

Having made a name for himself in the world of crime dramas thanks to his famous performance in The Wire as Jimmy McNulty, it's easy to see how the same skillset that made West a believable Baltimore detective could have also made him a solid 007 casting.

This concept is furthered by West being a standout actor more generally, as is in some ways symbolically reflected in his story about showing up in plain clothes for his audition, when other actors appeared in Bond-styled tuxes (as per Esquire).

The actor's comments that James Bond is "pretty much a superhero, and I think he should be more of a soldier" (via The Scotsman) do suggest West might have played his version of 007 in a different manner. While we've had no shortage of more high-octane Bonds, the idea of a "soldier Bond" speaks to a performance that could have been the spy's most grounded yet, which could have worked both for and against West and the franchise overall.

Michael Gambon's personal perspective on the matter of his James Bond audition is one that doesn't hold anything back. The actor stated to Ryan Tubridy on The Late Late Show that, when he was one of several stage actors interviewed for the role, he told them he didn't believe he was right for the role, commenting: "I can’t play James Bond, because I’m bald, I’ve got a double chin and I’ve got girl’s t*ts."

Given Michael Gambon's perspective on the matter, it's likely any more powerful James Bond performance from Gambon would've required either some major convincing of the actor, or otherwise a move to a less combat-based and perhaps more cerebral or cunning Bond, to allow Gambon to lean into the intellectual side of things in his performance.

Now that Anthony Starr is best known for playing a flying, superhuman villain who can shoot laser beams from his eyes in the form of The Boys' Homelander, it might seem surreal to consider him in the less fantastical - if still often over-the-top - world of spies as a prospective candidate for 007. However, this was indeed the case long before the actor appeared in The Boys, with Starr being one of the candidates for Casino Royale's Bond.

With Anthony Starr's audition for James Bond being one that has since leaked online, we have a direct vantage point into what Starr's spy could have looked like. Starr's smooth delivery and careful use of expression in this tape certainly suggest the actor could have nailed the role, and his filmography only supports the idea, as Anthony Starr has historically given his all to his roles.

Given Starr's greatest strength is his ability to imbue his characters with layered personalities that carry considerable depth, it does also seem more likely than not that his James Bond would have been one of the most three-dimensional feeling in the franchise's history, and may have felt far more individual in some senses than even other similar Bonds, crafting a distinct era for the hero.

Sam Worthington is another actor whose filmography makes it surreal to consider them as James Bond today. Worthington is now instead associated with the Avatar franchise and a decidedly different story of the blue Na'vi finding their way on their home planet of Pandora, rather than the human-centered stories James Bond naturally hinges around.

Regardless, Worthington was also another notable candidate for the role of Casino Royale's James Bond, presenting a very different story as to how his movie timeline could have unfurled. As it stands, Sam Worthington's history playing hardened roles looks as though it could have made him an engrossing Bond, with a kind of believable grit, and an everyman charm that could also make him a convincing spy in this sense.

Interestingly enough, though, it seems the real-world timeline is the actor's preferred one. Worthington noted in an interview with Australian radio station Nova’s Fitzy and Wippa (via Metro) that he was unsure of his delivery of James Bond's British accent, stating he "sounded like Dick Van Dyke from Mary Poppins", and also noted he felt he "just couldn’t get the charm" of the role down. Ultimately, this may mean his later success in the Avatar series instead was perhaps a better fit for the actor and franchise overall.

Inside Francesca Allen’s New Publication, Hair

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Last year, Francesca Allen brought Plaukai (Lithuanian for “hair”) to PhotoVogue Festival’s Women by Women, taking us into the world of a traditional long-hair competition in Lithuania. Now, she returns to the subject, continuing her exploration of the many ways women define themselves through hair.

For her new publication, Hair, Allen travelled to Las Vegas, where women from around the world gathered to compete for the inaugural title of Miss Rapunzel. She followed the contestants beyond the competition itself, from hotel rooms to casino floors and, finally, the stage where their hair is measured. Her photographs capture the curious rituals and unexpected intimacies of a pageant devoted entirely to hair.

Designed by Adam Ridgeway, Hair features a text by Claire Marie Healy and will be released as a limited edition in Paris on 12 November, during Paris Photo.

Francesca Allen’s practice spans photography and film, often centering on intimate depictions of girlhood and the complexities of coming of age. Her work has appeared in publications including AnOther, Dazed, i-D, M Le Monde, Vogue and ZEITmagazin, and has been exhibited at institutions such as the Grand Palais in Paris, BOZAR in Brussels, Somerset House and The Photographers’ Gallery in London. She is also the author of I’d Like to Get to Know You (2022) and Aya (2018). She lives and works in London.

Ravens adding QB Kedon Slovis off Packers' practice squad

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

With several quarterbacks injured on their 53-man roster, the Ravens have brought in another.

Per Tom Pelissero of Netflix, Baltimore is signing Kedon Slovis to its 53-man roster off of Green Bay’s practice squad.

Slovis, 25, has a connection to the Ravens’ staff through quarterbacks coach Israel Woolfork. He was Slovis’ position coach last season with the Cardinals.

Slovis made his pro debut last season with Arizona, appearing in two games. He attempted only two passes.

On Baltimore’s 53-man roster, Lamar Jackson is out indefinitely with a right thumb injury, Tyler Huntley was a non-participant on the Monday injury report with a toe issue, and Joe Fagnano was limited on Monday’s report with an ankle injury.

The team added Austin Reed to the practice squad at that position on Monday.

Baltimore will be at home to play the Jaguars on Thursday night.

Alan Ritchson's Dystopian Sci-Fi Becomes One Of The Biggest Movies On Streaming At The Perfect Time

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Alan Ritchson is one of the many stars of a dystopian sci-fi movie that has just found new life as one of the biggest releases on a major streaming service internationally, and it comes at the perfect time for the sci-fi franchise it is a part of. Ritchson's list of movies and TV shows has become defined by his turn as the physically imposing Jack Reacher in Prime Video's action thriller series Reacher and its new spin-off, Neagley. However, Ritchson has played with different genres throughout his career, including a fair share of comedies, superhero shows, and a few sci-fi releases.

In 2026, Ritchson starred in the sci-fi thriller War Machine, which has become the 10th most-watched Netflix movie of all time with 139.9 million views on its first 91 days, according to official Netflix data. That shows how the actor is a beloved genre star, and Ritchson appeared in multiple sci-fi releases before he got to lead the Netflix sensation. Among those are huge franchises, with Ritchson playing Raphael in two live-action Teenage Mutant Ninja Turtles movies. The actor also joined The Hunger Games dystopian sci-fi franchise with a role in 2013's The Hunger Games: Catching Fire, which has become a streaming hit.

At the time of writing, The Hunger Games: Catching Fire has become the 8th most popular movie on HBO Max's Top 10 Movies chart worldwide, according to data from FlixPatrol. The Hunger Games: Catching Fire was originally released in theaters on November 22, 2013, grossing a whopping $865 million by the end of its theatrical run, making it the biggest box office hit in the popular movie franchise. The Hunger Games: Catching Fire also boasts the highest Rotten Tomatoes critics' score for the dystopian sci-fi franchise with a stellar 90%. Catching Fire is streaming on Hulu, Lionsgate+, Tubi, and more in the United States, according to JustWatch.

Sign in to claim your place on the leaderboard!

Sign in to claim your place on the leaderboard!

On HBO Max internationally, The Hunger Games: Catching Fire ranks higher than Evil Dead Burn, which once topped the streamer's global charts. Ritchson's appearance in the beloved sci-fi franchise, where he plays the District 1 male tribute Gloss, is not the only The Hunger Games movie on the list. Alongside Catching Fire, The Hunger Games: Mockingjay - Part 1 ranks 10th, with the prequel The Hunger Games: The Ballad of Songbirds & Snakes at 7th, and the original The Hunger Games at 3rd, making the franchise a major hit on HBO Max globally. At the top of the chart is the Swedish horror movie Feed.

The Hunger Games franchise's streaming resurgence on HBO Max internationally is perfectly timed, as the sci-fi universe is coming back soon. Another The Hunger Games prequel movie, The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping, will be released in theaters on November 20. The movie explores a story fans have waited several years to see in live-action, as Sunrise on the Reaping is all about the Second Quarter Quell, aka The Hunger Games edition won by Haymitch Abernathy, with Joseph Zada as a young Haymitch. Franchise stars Jennifer Lawrence and Josh Hutcherson will return, so The Hunger Games franchise's streaming success sets up the new film nicely.

Johnny Depp on His One Condition to Return as Jack Sparrow for ‘Pirates of the Caribbean 6’

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Producer Jerry Bruckheimer has said he would welcome Depp to return to the role and had conversations with the actor about it.

Johnny Depp is open to reprising his role as Captain Jack Sparrow should Pirates of the Caribbean return but for one reason.

In an interview with Entertainment Tonight, the actor explained what would be his motivating factor to don the pirate hat again.

“If things are exactly where they need to be with regard to how they are going to top off the Pirates franchise… the only involvement I really want to have, truly, is just to send them off on a better note,” the actor said. He continued, “I will go to work with [Jerry] Bruckheimer tomorrow for anything. Doesn’t matter… if it’s Jerry, I trust him. We have a deep friendship.”

Depp headlined the five Pirates of the Caribbean films that released from 2003 to 2017.

Bruckheimer, who produced all five previous films, previously told The Hollywood Reporter in 2022 that they were “getting very close” with Pirates 6 and even had a “very good script.” The producer also noted that Pirates would be rebooted “so that is easier to put together“ given “you don’t have to wait for certain actors.”

The last Pirates of the Caribbean movie, Dead Men Tell No Tales, was released was in 2017 and starred Depp, Javier Bardem and Kaya Scodelario, as well as returning franchise cast members Geoffrey Rush, Orlando Bloom, and Keira Knightley.

Bruckheimer has been candid about work on a new Pirates film which would have Jeff Nathanson writing the script. Another reported film, penned by Christina Hodson from Birds of Prey, was to star Margot Robbie. In her Vanity Fair 2022 cover story Robbie said of the film, “We had an idea and we were developing it for a while, ages ago, to have more of a female-led — not totally female-led, but just a different kind of story — which we thought would’ve been really cool, but I guess they don’t want to do it.”

However, Bruckheimer has said he still intended on getting a reboot and Robbie‘s film made: “We hope to get ’em both made, and I think Disney agrees they really want to make the Margot one, too,” Bruckheimer told Entertainment Weekly.

“I think he’s cracked it,” Bruckheimer also told EW of Nathanson’s script. “He’s got an amazing third act. We just gotta clean up the first and second and then we’ll get there. But he wrote a great, great third act.”

Bruckheimer previously told EW that he would welcome Depp to return to the role and had conversations with the actor about it. “It’s a reboot, but if it was up to me, he would be in it,” Bruckheimer said. “I love him. He’s a good friend. He’s an amazing artist, and he’s a unique look. He created Captain Jack. That was not on the page; that was him doing a little Pepé Le Pew and Keith Richards. That was his interpretation of Jack Sparrow.”

Nicole Taylor, Singer of Triplets Band Taylor Red, Dead at 35

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Nicole Taylor—who formed the band Taylor Red with her identical twin sisters Nika Taylor and Natalie Taylor—has died at age 35: “We are shocked and heartbroken.”

Nicole Taylor, who was a member of the country music trio alongside triplet sisters Nika Taylor and Natalie Taylor, died on Sept. 28 following a battle with breast cancer, her siblings confirmed. She was 35.

“We are shocked and heartbroken to announce the passing of our sister Nicole Taylor. This isn’t the video we thought we’d ever be making,” Natalie said beside Nika in a video shared to YouTube Nov. 2. "We're still processing everything. This honestly feels really hard to talk about, but we're going to do our best in sharing some of her story.”

Though the sisters formed Taylor Red—formerly known as Red Roots—in 2011, they gained popularity in 2021 after their song “Country Money” went viral on social media.

But Natalie and Nika shared that one year later, Nicole had been diagnosed with "very aggressive" stage three breast cancer.

And while they’d hoped for a positive outcome following their sister’s 2022 diagnosis, Nika shared that they’ve found peace in knowing that their sister is “no longer suffering.”

“We thought she would be here sharing her story of earthly healing,” Nika explained, “but instead we’re here sharing her story of heavenly healing.”

Both Natalie and Nika recalled being with their older sister when she received her diagnosis.

"I just remember breaking down in absolute shock,” Natalie said. “I was just so surprised. We've been blessed with good health our whole life so this was a big shock and it wasn't what we were expecting.”

She continued, "I was crying and ran out to the hallway to tell Nika and I just remember Nicole said, 'God's going to use this for his glory.'"

Despite responding well to treatment and being told at one point she had "no evidence of the disease," Nicole's breast cancer returned and had progressed to stage four, which is when she was given just three months to live.

But Nicole beat those odds with “fearlessness,” living another year before she died last month. And in the face of tragedy, Natalie shared her faith has only strengthened since the loss.

“I've been a Christian a long time and something like this, you know, can make you either grow closer to God or distance yourself,” Natalie said. “This trial I would say grows that. I don’t know how someone could get through this kind of thing without God.”

The sisters confirmed on their website that the family held a private funeral service, adding that “Nicole, the oldest of us three sisters, will be greatly missed.”

“She was our leader and our best friend,” they added in the statement. “We are heartbroken, but we know she is in Heaven and will see her again.”

Iconic James Bond Actors Give Official Verdict On Rumored New 007 Star

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The James Bond franchise has had many variations over the years, all originating from British author Ian Fleming's novels, where he introduced 007 in his 1953 novel Casino Royale. The film franchise began with Albert R. "Cubby" Broccoli and Harry Saltzman, who purchased the screen rights and created Eon Productions in 1961, with the first movie, Dr. No, arriving in 1962. Now, Amazon is taking on the legendary property, and the race for the next Bond is on.

Pierce Brosnan and Judi Dench have given their thoughts on the franchise, including the upcoming Amazon MGM Studios adaptation and who should get the shaken-and-not-stirred Martini. In an interview with Vanity Fair, the former 007, Brosnan, weighed in on the hotly debated casting battle between British actors for the new Bond movies. Brosnan backed his favorite candidate and stated:

"Oh, I’m just an audience member now. I’m just the man in the street who is eager to see who is going to play this epic role of cinematic history. All the men who are on the list now are actors I admire. Callum Turner is someone I’ve worked with, and he’s so strong. They all have a voice, a physique, and a presence on film. They all have longevity as actors, all of these young men."

Brosnan played James Bond in four Eon Productions films from 1995 to 2002, and helped to revive the franchise after a six-year hiatus. He was initially offered the role of the British secret agent in 1986, but had to fulfill his obligations with a TV series titled Remington Steele. Timothy Dalton took on the role for the two movies, The Living Daylights and Licence to Kill. Brosnan portrayed Bond in GoldenEye, Tomorrow Never Dies, The World Is Not Enough, and Die Another Day.

Brosnan also voiced, and his likeness was scanned for James Bond video games, including the Nintendo 64 title GoldenEye 007. He later exited the role as Bond and paved the way for Daniel Craig to take it on in 2006. Brosnan backing Turner, known for Eternity, is a surprising turn of events due to Slow Horses's star Jack Lowden being the usual top choice according to many betting sites and ScreenRant's own poll.

Dench is known for portraying James Bond's MI5 boss M in eight Bond movies, which began with GoldenEye (1995) and ended with Skyfall (2012), as well as a brief cameo in Spectre (2015). In an exclusive excerpt from the iconic actress's memoir Is It Too Late to Make a Run For It?, Variety has shared some snippets where Dench reflects on her career in the Bond franchise. She also reveals her favorite Bond, and how the character's evolution reflects the times:

"I’ll always have a soft spot for Sean [Connery] because he was the original, but what’s lovely is that both Pierce and Daniel really made it their own. They each have a great sense of humor and can be self-deprecating, which I find very attractive in life, and which I think is an essential quality for Bond.

What I like is the way the character molds around the actor – so much so that I can’t then imagine anybody else playing the part. When I first played M, Bond was suave and elegant. He’d order his martinis and pass through fights almost effortlessly, emerging with hardly a scratch on his face or a mark on his Savile Row suit. Then gradually – over the course of the eight films I did – Bond became dirtier, more realistic, less of a fantasy figure, I suppose. I don’t think the scripts are as witty as they once were, which is perhaps a shame, but then the character moves on – Bond reflects the times."

Raiders to determine RB work by "who’s playing the best ball at the time"

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

When the Raiders drafted Ashton Jeanty with the sixth overall pick in 2025, the expectation was that he’d be their top choice in the backfield for years to come.

Jeanty has taken the most snaps of any of the team’s running backs so far this season, but he averaged just over three yards a carry in the first seven games so the Raiders changed things up in Week 8. Mike Washington got more snaps than Jeanty and carried the ball 25 times while Jeanty ran it twice. Jeanty saw most of his playing time on passing downs and came up with a key 45-yard catch and run, but the shift in playing time remained a topic of conversation at head coach Klint Kubiak’s Monday press conference.

Kubiak said it wasn’t a permanent arrangement because decisions on playing time will be based on who the team feels is the top option at a given moment.

“I think it’s always a moving target,” Kubiak said. “You see where your team is at and who’s playing the best ball at the time. It’s a 17-game regular season where things change every week. They’ve been changing at guard, they change up front defensively, they change at corner. That’s the NFL. We have a young roster and we’re going to give guys opportunities and see what fits us best. But your 11, whether you’re playing really well or not playing well at all, it’s always going to change a little bit.”

Kubiak pointed to Washington’s practice work rather than Jeanty’s sluggish production as the reason why he got bumped up in Sunday’s rotation. Washington produced 83 yards on 25 carries, so it wasn’t a major change in terms of success on the ground for the Raiders. It was a win after four straight losses, though, and that’s usually a good argument for sticking with an approach the next time out.

Now, for the first time, Ritchie and Statham are bringing their action prowess to Netflix for a revenge-focused action vehicle.

Netflix has now officially announced that Guy Ritchie and Jason Statham will re-team for Dogfight, a revenge thriller "with a stylish twist" set in the criminal underworld of London. Dogfight marks Guy Ritchie's second collaboration with Netflix after his hit crime thriller series The Gentlemen, which serves as a spinoff of Ritchie's 2019 movie of the same name. Christina Bowlby Finds Inspiration Close to Home for Bentley Mills

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

For textile designer Christina Bowlby, inspiration can come from an unexpected place: the warm, heathered coats of her two Aussiedoodles, Birdie and Maggie. The dogs have become unlikely muses for Coming Home, a new carpet collection from Bentley Mills that coincides with Bowlby’s first year as the company’s senior director of design—and her return to California.

Christina Bowlby with her Aussiedoodles, Birdie and Maggie. Photography courtesy of Bentley Mills.

Bowlby and her two dogs relocated to Los Angeles nearly a year ago, trading Chattanooga, Tennessee, for the West Coast. It’s a homecoming for Bowlby, who grew up in California. Although she quickly fell in love with the South after a day trip and a ghost tour, she always imagined returning west.

Originally a film major at the Savannah College of Art and Design (SCAD), she discovered her interest in textiles almost by accident. “I took a fiber 101 class, and it changed my life,” she says. She entered the carpet industry upon graduation and never looked back.

“I thought Chattanooga was my forever home,” she recalls, but an opportunity at Bentley changed her plans. “The food, the culture, the people of LA—there is always something to do. The beach, mountains, of course Bentley; it has everything.”

Birdie and Maggie have settled into their new surroundings, too— their coats soon found their way into Bowlby’s design work. As she researched color trends for the new collection, she began noticing shades that reminded her of the dogs’ distinctive coloring.

Two of the collection’s four colorways draw directly from the Aussiedoodles. Birdie’s warm golden coat inspired Cactus Wren, named for a bird native to the American Southwest, while Maggie’s softer mix of colors informed Pure Magic, a nod to the dog’s full name.

The other two colorways, Arroyo Moon and Old Pasadena, reference the Southern California landscape. Collectively, the earthy neutral palette draws on sun-washed sands, stone, clay-infused taupes, and muted moss. Drawing on Bowlby’s experience in hospitality design, the collection favors warmth and tonal complexity over sharp contrast, with an emphasis on creating spaces that feel calm and comfortable.

The collection’s early mood boards reinforce that direction, pairing lived-in interiors and natural textures with images of coastal landscapes. Warm woods, soft furnishings, and muted colors establish a relaxed, residential sensibility that carries through to the finished carpets.

“The yarns for this collection—Helix® premium nylon 6,6 and Revolve® technologies from Universal Fibers—were such a good vehicle for translating [the color inspiration] because it has this heathered, wool-like quality,” Bowlby explains. “I was able to choose the specific hues and combine them in a way that looks natural. They were the perfect match for this collection.”

The heathered yarns allow multiple hues to mingle within a single surface, creating tonal variation reminiscent of natural fibers. This approach is particularly suited to Bowlby’s palette, where closely related shades of beige, taupe, gray, and brown create depth without relying on strong color contrasts.

The patterns introduce another layer of visual texture. Fragmented linear forms, overlapping markings, and irregular geometric elements create movement across the surface, while variations in density give the designs a sense of dimension. The result is a collection that feels visually layered without overwhelming its subdued palette.

The Coming Home palette is intended to feel enduring rather than tied to a particular moment. Bowlby credits this approach to her habit of noticing color in everyday surroundings, even when the source seems unremarkable. She recalls taking roughly 15 photographs of a stone wall to capture its variations for future design work. “It had the most beautiful range of warm neutrals I’ve seen in a while,” she says.

That attention to overlooked details connects Bowlby’s personal inspirations with her work at Bentley, where familiar surroundings can become the starting point for something new. “I share that ‘wink and a nod’ approach to design and life,” she says of Bentley.

As for Maggie and Birdie, their modeling careers may have peaked with their carpet-colorway debuts. For now, they’re back to hiking, beach runs, and making friends wherever they go. If you happen to spot them on Venice Beach, don’t be surprised if Birdie comes over to say hello.

From Drifting Lights to Crystal Grid, Preciosa’s extensive design portfolio brings the craft to customizable crystal.

Beyond screens: Mercedes-Benz's new holographic heads-up display

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

After pioneering the passenger screen, Mercedes has designs on the windshield.

Do screens in cars represent luxury? Judging by the latest car interiors, the answer would seem to be an emphatic “yes.” Screens aren’t just getting larger; they are multiplying across the dashboard and morphing into different forms and concepts. Indeed, an interior’s screens are a pretty good indicator of what brand made the car—or at least how much the owner paid for it.

But is this actually what customers want? Are more screens really better? And even if they are, luxury car features inevitably trickle down to cheaper, more mainstream offerings, and screens can only get so big. That leaves luxury automakers with another question to answer: “What’s next?”

Mercedes-Benz is working on a new display alternative that could provide an answer. For now, it’s known as the holographic display. Forget the images from Star Trek and Star Wars that just popped into your head, though. It’s effectively a more advanced form of heads-up display, or HUD.

Whereas a traditional HUD projects images onto the windshield glass, the holographic display has two main components. It still uses a projector, but its key element is a special transparent film laminated into the glass. The film uses holographic optical elements, or “super-small mirrors,” as Mercedes development engineer Lena Hees described them, that selectively reflect the light emitted by the projector, keeping the image visible even in direct sunlight while hiding it from the driver’s view.

Today, the passenger-side touchscreens in Mercedes and other luxury vehicles rely on driver-monitoring cameras or screen polarization to keep the driver from seeing what is playing on the passenger side.

Speaking of passenger screens, the holographic display is clearly being considered as a future alternative or replacement for this technology. Although screens in cars are increasingly ubiquitous, a recent JD Power study indicated that car owners classify passenger screens as “not necessary” and have generally negative opinions about them. Mercedes has clearly observed similar opinions but for slightly different reasons.

“We have customers who like the Hyperscreen,” said development engineer Maximilian Hilger of the pillar-to-pillar screen found in an increasing number of Mercedes models. “But there are others who want to maintain luxury materials.”

In other words, the massive screen design doesn’t seem as luxurious as the pillar-to-pillar bands of wood or other materials found in previous-generation models. And that’s not an opinion isolated to Mercedes’ customers.

New Mercedes chief design officer Bastian Baudy recently told German magazine Auto Motor Sport that “Materiality and haptics must remain tangible—with switches, buttons, and intuitive controls. Real materials are at the heart of Mercedes: metal, wood.”

Mercedes CEO Ola Källenius went further, grouping interior quality and materials among the brand’s “forever values,” along with safety and maintaining what a Mercedes is like to drive.

That presents conflicting priorities, however.

“Yes, you need to have all the [technology] that a customer could ever dream of and wants,” Källenius told Ars. “But you need to package it in an aesthetically pleasing way. That’s also part of it.”

That brings us back to the holographic display. Keep in mind that the photos in this article show early prototypes I saw at Mercedes’ research facilities in Sindelfingen, Germany. The prototype projector sits inside a rather crude, trapezoidal hole dug into the dash of a right-hand-drive, pre-2027 S-Class. It’s also loud, with a cooling fan whirring like a laptop visiting too many junk-filled websites.

The production version would have a much smaller projector opening than those of the prototype and existing heads-up displays. Manual Holstein, a manager working in Mercedes research for future displays, indicated that the projector could be small enough to be hidden behind wood or metal trim pieces. Holstein also said you won’t hear a fan in the production model and that the resolution would improve.

The resolution is certainly considerably better than any current head-up display, including Mercedes’ own, which features augmented reality elements. Full-color, commercial-like videos of the C-Class Electric played during the demonstration, and the display was sufficiently bright, even though Holstein said the prototype was operating at just 20 percent of its maximum brightness.

Issues remain, however. The resolution may be good compared to the arrows, script, and graphics of a current HUD, but it’s poor when compared to a passenger touchscreen. The video’s contrast was also too high, and as you can see in the photos, the picture was distorted into a narrow height due to the angle of the windshield. Frankly, no one would choose to watch anything on it over a phone, tablet, or conventional passenger screen. It needs to improve by orders of magnitude before becoming a viable alternative.

Again, though, this is just a prototype. Holstein said the holographic film size, or “eyebox,” can be made much bigger and be raised higher than the HUD-like height of the prototype. He also dismissed the distortion as a problem unique to the prototype.

Imagine nearly half the windshield transformed into a virtual screen that’s visible only to the passenger. Beyond videos or other entertainment, Mercedes envisions the holographic display highlighting points of interest in the view through the windshield. A similar display could be placed on the driver side, showing situational information, enabling video calls, or providing entertainment when parked. In the future, it could even let drivers watch things other than the road when Mercedes’ Level 3 autonomous driving system allows them to take their eyes off it. At the very least, the technology could replace today’s HUDs and their larger projector boxes.

Having experienced the prototype while the car was moving, I also think the holographic display could help prevent the motion sickness that can come from staring at a passenger screen while the car is moving. The higher screen angle and ability to see more of the outside world beyond created a notably different visual experience.

Of course, the question remains: “Is this what customers want?”

If the JD Power survey found that customers don’t care about passenger touchscreens, will they be any more enthusiastic about holographic video displays? That seems doubtful. But that’s just as much of an open question as whether the production version will actually live up to its promises.

There’s also another matter: What, exactly, defines luxury? Before Bastian Baudy took over as Mercedes’ chief design officer this year, his predecessor and former boss Gordon Wagener echoed Källenius’ thoughts about the direction of the brand’s interiors. He was considerably blunter, though.

“Screens are not luxury,” Wagener told ABC News. “You have a better—and bigger—TV at home, right? And every car has a big screen. So we have to create luxury beyond the screen. That’s why I talk about craftsmanship and sophistication.”

If the holographic display lets automakers emphasize traditional design and materials while still offering unique technology, it could be a promising way forward.

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

Parties That Changed the World: Barbie’s Premiere and Gone With the Wind’s Debut

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The world’s most famous doll was inspired by a spunky, high-end call girl named Lilli. Described as a “gold digger, exhibitionist, and floozy,” the character of Bild Lilli was created in 1952 by German cartoonist Reinhard Beuthien after he was asked to draw a filler cartoon for the inaugural issue of the tabloid Bild-Zeitung. Lilli, a buxom blonde with a perky ponytail and sky-high stilettos, sat beside a fortune teller. “Can’t you give me the name and address of this tall, handsome, rich man?” she cooed. The cartoon was an instant hit, and in 1955, Lilli was immortalized in plastic.

A year later, Ruth Handler discovered the Lilli doll while on a vacation in Europe. She bought several. One was for her teenage daughter, Barbara, to keep; the rest were brought back to Mattel, the toy company she had cofounded with her husband, Elliot, and businessman Harold Matson. (Mattel is a portmanteau of both men’s names.) Lilli—with comically large breasts, chic outfits, and heavy makeup—was precisely what Handler had been looking for.

Robin Gerber, author of Barbie and Ruth, wrote: “The doll looked too much like a prostitute for the company’s taste,” so Mattel’s design team (slightly) softened Lilli’s exaggerated proportions, renamed her Barbie, after Barbara, and before long, she would become the physical ideal for generations of young girls and women everywhere.

Sixty-four years after Barbie hit shelves, she finally got her close-up in Barbie, a feminist manifesto in a pair of pink stilettos. The film’s premiere and after-party at the Shrine Auditorium marked Warner Bros.’ largest premiere to date—and the cherry on top of a bubble-gum-flavored, years-long marketing blitz. According to Event Marketer, “the affair was like Barbie herself: high-energy, glamorous and very, very, colorful.”

It was a beautiful day in Hollywood. Even by Los Angeles standards, the weather on Sunday, July 9, 2023, was almost impossibly perfect—sunshine, low 70s, and a cool breeze—as if Hollywood itself has been dropped into the fantasy world of Barbie Land.

The event kicked off with a splash. The pink carpet was tricked out with replicas of pieces from the film’s beach scenes: a lifeguard station, a towering lighthouse, faux palm trees, and a 3D ocean wave, with carpeting that shifted colors to mimic sand. A wide range of influencers were invited to get first dibs on photo ops before the stars arrived. But the real showstopper was the two-story replica of Barbie’s Dreamhouse, complete with a 2D faux pool, a DJ spinning tracks, and the trademark spiral slide. “We had to have that slide,” Wendy Robino, SVP of Warner Bros. marketing events, said at the time.

On Sunday, they wore pink: Writer and director Greta Gerwig, along with cast members Issa Rae, America Ferrera, Kate McKinnon, Ryan Gosling, and Michael Cera, turned out for the premiere in every shade of Barbie’s signature color, turning something long dismissed as girly into a celebration of individuality, joy, and girl power.

But the belle of the ball wasn’t one to blend in. After weeks of thinking pink, Margot Robbie—the film’s star and producer—arrived in black. Her custom Schiaparelli look re-created 1960s’ Solo in the Spotlight Barbie: a black sequined dress with a single red rose stitched onto her tulle skirt, matching opera gloves, Manolo Blahnik mules, and a diamond necklace. Movie Star Barbie fully committed to the bit, even clutching a pink handkerchief as she posed in front of a pink Corvette. Robbie’s blond hair was swept into a high ponytail—reminiscent of the one first worn by Lilli.

Following the official screening—marking the first time Barbie was revealed to the public—it was time to party.

The theme was “Barbie’s Blowout Disco Party.” Fittingly, one attraction invited guests inside a glittering, 12-foot pink disco ball. Justin Lefkovitch, whose company Mirrored Media, partnered with Mattel to create it, tells VF the entrance created “a shroud of mystery” for partygoers. Inside, a six-foot 1:1 Dreamhouse showcasing the new Barbie the Movie doll collection was surrounded by gold and silver streamers and over a dozen custom pink disco balls. The goal, Lefkovitch recalls, was to “transport guests into the world of Barbie.”

Nicki Minaj was the first guest to arrive at the party, event producer Austin Johnston tells VF—“five-foot-long hair all the way down to the floor.” A theme park designer by trade, Johnston and his company, AKJohnston, transformed the after-party into what he calls a “pop-up museum.” Barbie Land’s pastel-colored cul-de-sac was re-created as “a 65-foot-tall, 70-foot-wide” inflatable, its landmarks crammed together like a kitschy postcard. At the end of Barbie’s driveway, Mark Ronson presided over a hot pink DJ booth, playing hits from the soundtrack by Ice Spice, Dua Lipa (a.k.a. Mermaid Barbie), and Nicki Minaj—whom Johnston had to “very kindly” ask to get off Barbie’s Corvette.

Tucked beneath the second-floor mezzanine, a series of alcoves became larger-than-life photo sets, with photographers on hand to capture guests in action. Partygoers could retrace Barbie’s journey to the Real World—by bike, boat, and rocket ship—or step inside the Kendom man cave, Weird Barbie’s house, and life-size Barbie and Ken toy boxes. Not everyone hired to re-create Barbie Land had actually seen the movie by then; only a handful were invited to be part of that “really restricted small group,” Johnston says.

At the center of the room, guests sipped pink cocktails under a giant disco ball while an 11-foot, glitter-coated pink B twirled above. Another bar doubled as a fashion show, with four life-size mannequins dolled up for disco in original costumes from the film’s dance number, including Barbie’s gold sequined jumpsuit and the Kens’ matching white-and-gold tracksuits.

Obviously, the food was on theme too. Wolfgang Puck catered the evening’s Barbie-Q, serving grilled salmon, summer melon salad, roasted Pink Fir apple potatoes, char siu glazed grilled chicken, and charred tricolor cauliflower.

Even the night’s corporate sponsors knew better than to steal Barbie’s spotlight. OPI set up a nail salon, and Xbox provided a gaming station where players had their pick of two General Motors rides: Barbie’s Corvette or Ken’s Hummer. A highlight for Johnston was spotting Robbie racing a virtual Hummer while her Australian parents cheered her on. “No one paid her to do that,” he says. “She’s just like, I want to have a drink and play a video game at my premiere.”

For one night in Hollywood, women could be anything. And 84 years earlier, another city had hosted another rager in honor of another larger-than-life heroine. While Los Angeles turned pink for Barbie, Atlanta resurrected the Old South for Scarlett O’Hara.

All hell broke loose when British actor Vivien Leigh was cast as beloved heroine Scarlett O’Hara in the big-screen adaptation of Margaret Mitchell’s epic, Gone With the Wind. Fans and journalists were outraged that such an iconic Southern Belle would be played by a foreigner. At the time, Leigh was yet to star in a Hollywood film and was best known stateside for her affair with Laurence Olivier. Gossip columnist Hedda Hopper sniped: “Out of millions of American women, [producer David O. Selznick] couldn’t find one to suit him.”

Hopper was exaggerating, but not by a lot: During a grueling two-year nationwide search for his leading lady, Selznick reportedly met with over 1,400 actresses. After angry fan letters poured in, Selznick’s publicity machine kicked into overdrive—and eventually, the public came around to the idea that a refined Brit was somehow easier to swallow than a Yankee.

But perhaps the most convincing endorsement came from Leigh’s own leading man. Even Clark Gable, who initially considered Leigh “too demure” for the Southern firecracker, was won over by her dedication and declared she “was Scarlett every minute.” His own casting, meanwhile, had been a dream come true from the thousands who flooded MGM and Selznick’s offices with letters demanding he play Rhett Butler

The shoot was famously plagued by drama, and by the time filming wrapped, the cast and crew had earned themselves a party.

But Selznick had bigger fish to fry—namely, finishing the movie in time for its premiere. He left the party planning to local organizers and his trusted story editor Kay Brown, who had convinced her boss to buy the film rights in the first place. But since Atlanta was still tightly in the grip of Jim Crow, not everyone could join in. Leigh’s costars Hattie McDaniel and Butterfly McQueen were excluded from the festivities.

Selznick argued, unsuccessfully, with local organizers to get McDaniel on the guest list. Gable threatened to not attend the premiere in solidarity with his friend, The Hollywood Reporter stated, but McDaniel ultimately talked him out of it. “While it was unfortunate to exclude Mammy, it was the wisest policy,” Brown wrote, per Matthew Bernstein, former professor of film and media studies at Emory University.

Meanwhile, white Atlanta was gearing up for the party of the century. Former president Jimmy Carter later called it the South’s “biggest event” in his lifetime. According to Time, then governor Eurith D. Rivers was prepared to call in the National Guard, while Mayor Hartsfield urged every woman of Atlanta to dust off her hoop skirts and each man to “sprout a goatee, sideburns, and Kentucky colonel whiskers.”

Hollywood came in hot. Leigh and Gable made their grand entrance by limousine, waving to the 300,000 spectators who lined seven miles of streets in downtown Atlanta—including a number of nonagenarian Confederate veterans who were invited as special guests. Leigh arrived with Olivier while Gable brought his new wife, Carole Lombard. Fun fact: Gable reportedly hadn’t even wanted to play Rhett Butler until Louis B. Mayer offered to help bankroll his divorce so he could marry Lombard.

Of the motorcade, Time wrote: “Crowds larger than the combined armies that fought at Atlanta in July 1864 waved Confederate flags, tossed confetti till it seemed to be snowing, gave three different versions of the Rebel yell, whistled, cheered, goggled.” Leigh wept as the crowd welcomed the British actress “back home,” as if she’d just returned from battle.

The multiday event included a costume ball thrown by the Junior League that would have Abraham Lincoln rolling over in his grave. Decked out in hoop skirts, bustles, and Confederate-era uniforms, 6,000 guests descended on the Municipal Auditorium, which had been transformed into a recreation of the film’s Monster Bazaar. Black participants were relegated to serving or entertaining. Among them was the Ebenezer Baptist Church choir, whose singers reportedly performed dressed as enslaved people in front of a façade of Tara, a fictional plantation from the film. One of the singers was 10-year-old future civil rights hero Martin Luther King Jr.

Vivien Leigh arrived in a velvet gown designed by Walter Plunkett. Though Olivier escorted her costar Olivia de Havilland instead—to keep up appearances—Leigh was the belle of the ball, especially in hometown hero Margaret Mitchell’s absence. Eighteen years earlier, the young novelist had performed a risqué “apache dance” at a charity gala, which cost her her Junior League membership. Now, with Hollywood celebrating her masterpiece, some suspected Mitchell was getting the last laugh by snubbing the biggest party in town.

Young women fangirled all over Gable. According to Time, one woman fainted at the sight of him. Another was given a choice: a Christmas gift or a chance to meet the heartthrob. She chose Gable. After he kissed the 11-year-old, she gasped, “Now am I a woman?”

Governor Rivers declared December 15, 1939, a statewide holiday in honor of Gone With the Wind’s world premiere, the grand finale of the three-day extravaganza. Searchlights swept the Southern sky, and flashbulbs popped as 18,000 spectators crowded Peachtree Street. Even Loew’s Grand Theater got a glow-up. Its Romanesque façade was disguised as a grand Southern plantation—four towering white columns were erected to support a temporary façade with floral garlands, potted trees, and an enormous portrait of Leigh and Gable hung above the entrance.

Inside, all 2,000-plus seats sold out at $10 a pop—the equivalent of about $240 today. That’s not exactly chump change, considering Atlanta was still licking its wounds after the Great Depression. Young women in antebellum costumes greeted guests before Mayor Hartsfield took the stage to kick things off. Selznick and the film’s stars received standing ovations that lasted a full minute. But the loudest cheers were for Mitchell, now in attendance, who joined Leigh and Gable onstage.

The lights dimmed for nearly four hours of melodrama. When Rhett Butler delivered his immortal parting shot—“Frankly, my dear, I don’t give a damn”—the audience reportedly gasped, then erupted in applause as he left Scarlett in his dust.

Before Gone With the Wind—still the highest-grossing film of all time when adjusted for inflation—premieres were usually exclusive screenings in Hollywood or New York. 1939’s antebellum extravaganza turned an entire city into a movie set, setting the stage for modern spectacles like Barbie.

Both parties also offer a revealing snapshot of the societies that threw them. For Gone With the Wind, the industry glorified an imagined past; for Barbie, Hollywood toasted an imagined future. The Barbie premiere wasn’t just a love letter to a feminist film: It was also a giant corporate juggernaut designed to sell a product whose history was tangled up with the very stereotypes the film was questioning. It paid off: Following the film’s theatrical release, Mattel reported a third-quarter increase in worldwide gross billings of 27%, and Barbie toy sales in the United States increased 25% for the July-August combined months compared to the same two-month period a year prior. The immersive premiere, brand collaborations, worldwide activations, and fan participation blurred the line between marketing a movie and creating a cultural event.

The film also became Warner Bros.’ most successful release of all time, grossing $1.4 billion worldwide and making Greta Gerwig the first solo female director admitted into the hallowed halls of the billion-dollar boys club. The premiere celebrated a movie that changed the cultural conversation while handily outgrossing the testosterone-fueled Oppenheimer—a spectacular rebuke to the assumption that chick flicks have a glass ceiling.

Its timing made the celebration particularly potent. In that moment, Hollywood was still finding its footing after the pandemic; the premiere offered something audiences were missing. “It eventized going back to the movies. It eventized the premiere,” says Lefkovitch.

For one night, partygoers got to revel in a fairy tale where women ruled the world. But outside Barbie Land, the irony had all the subtlety of Ken’s Mojo Dojo Casa House. Hollywood was throwing an enormous party celebrating a fantasy of female empowerment while women were still fighting for equality in the real world. There is “definitely some hypocrisy,” agrees Lefkovitch, who made a point of thanking his all-female team.

Decades earlier, Atlanta had thrown its own enormous party, celebrating a fantasy of Southern womanhood while reinforcing the racial and social hierarchies of the real South—and conveniently leaving the horrors of slavery out of the picture. (The film itself did just that, presenting a fictional plantation where the most loyal individuals enjoyed being enslaved.) Black performers were welcome to entertain white audiences, but not to celebrate alongside them; not a single seat inside Loew’s Grand Theater was available to Black moviegoers.

Together, these indignities galvanized the local Black press. In a column for Atlanta Daily World, Reverend John Clarence Wright wrote, “As Atlanta goes mad with the Wind one hundred thousand of her citizens should become more thoughtful than ever of the course that must be pursued to gain their rights as free men and citizens in a land where the spirit of secession and slavery still lives.” With Black moviegoers barred from Southern theaters, the Pittsburgh Courier dryly observed, “Negro reaction… will have to wait until the film comes North.”

The premiere and the film’s success helped propel the NAACP to fight not just individual films, but toward a broader campaign to change Hollywood itself. In 1942, NAACP leader Walter White spoke directly to studio heads in an attempt to persuade them to steer away from demeaning Black stereotypes—like the ones onscreen in Gone With the Wind—and to increase the roles available to Black actors in Hollywood.

White’s approach infuriated the very woman whose absence from the Atlanta premiere had helped expose Hollywood’s hypocrisy: Hattie McDaniel. As a Black woman, McDaniel’s choices were limited—“I’d rather play a maid than be one,” she once said. Writing for Vanity Fair, Hadley Hall Meares noted that McDaniel was “incensed” that White had negotiated with the studios on her behalf, and believed she and her fellow Black actors deserved their own seat at the negotiating table.

McDaniel did get a seat at the 1940 Oscars—but it was at a small table at the edge of the room, separated from her Gone With the Wind costars. The ceremony, after all, was held at the Cocoanut Grove nightclub, which had a strict color line. When her name was called, McDaniel gushed: “I sincerely hope I shall always be a credit to my race and to the motion picture industry.”

Meares described McDaniel’s life as “a tightrope walk.” She was expected to satisfy the white executives who controlled her career, the Black audiences who wanted better representation, and her own ambitions as an actress.

Vivien Leigh also made history that night, becoming the first Brit to win the Oscar for best actress. She dedicated her award to Selznick for his “energy, courage, and very great kindness.” After all, he had been partly responsible for convincing an initially outraged public that Leigh was worthy to play their beloved Scarlett O’Hara. Years later, the Academy lavished Barbie with eight nominations, though it snubbed Gerwig for best director and Robbie for best actress. (Frankly, my dear, they didn’t give a damn about the two women who created Barbie Land.) If Hollywood could build an entire universe in which women ruled the world, why was it still so difficult to imagine one outside the movie theater?

Billie Eilish and Finneas O’Connell took home Barbie’s only Oscar, for best original song. Their winning composition poses Barbie’s existential question while echoing the expectations heaped upon Scarlett, Leigh, McDaniel, Robbie, and Gerwig: “What Was I Made For?”

It turns out the inspiration for Barbie—the quintessential American toy—wasn’t even made for little girls. Lilli dolls were sold to grown men in European tobacco shops and adult toy stores, becoming popular gag gifts. “Men… put them on their car dashboard, dangled them from the rearview mirror, or gave them to girlfriends as a suggestive keepsake,” Gerber wrote. Long before Barbie could be anything, she was something for men.

Eighty-four years apart, Hollywood threw two spectacular parties for women who refused to play by the rules. But the rules were still very much in place when the lights came back up.

Jason Statham, Guy Ritchie Set Reunion on Netflix’s Revenge Action Film ‘Dogfight’

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Ritchie will write the movie, described as a "stylish actioner set in the London criminal underworld."

Jason Statham and Guy Ritchie are reuniting on a new film for Netflix, Dogfight.

Production on Dogfight, from Black Bear, Toff Guy Films and Punch Palace, is set to start in early 2027, with filming set to take place in the U.K. Plot details remain under wraps, but the streamer describes it as “a stylish revenge actioner set in the London criminal underworld.”

Written by the celebrated British director, the movie marks the seventh collaboration between him and Statham. Their partnership includes the likes of Lock, Stock and Two Smoking Barrels, Snatch, and the upcoming Viva La Madness. Related Stories Movies British Independent Film Awards: 'Elsinore' Leads Nominations With 12 Lifestyle Marilyn Monroe, Audrey Hepburn, PÖFF Garments Bring the Glitz to New Fashion Museum Near Tallinn

Ritchie and Statham are both producers on Dogfight, alongside Ivan Atkinson and John Friedberg.

The writer-filmmaker is booked and busy. He created Netflix’s hit crime series The Gentlemen, led by Theo James and based on Ritchie’s 2019 movie of the same name. The show nabbed headlines recently with reports that Meghan Markle, the Duchess of Sussex, was eyeing a role in the series. She was later apparently dropped from consideration.

Ritchie also serves as a director and executive producer on Paramount+’s MobLand, starring Tom Hardy, Pierce Brosnan and Helen Mirren. Rumored disarray on that set also made headlines following speculation around Hardy’s firing, but the tension has seemingly been squashed with Hardy’s season three return confirmed. Young Sherlock for Amazon Prime Video, featuring Hero Fiennes Tiffin as the titular detective, is another of Ritchie’s directing and EPing gigs.

Statham, meanwhile, is a real-life action man. He’s best known for his team-ups with Ritchie, as well as appearances in the Transporter and Fast & Furious franchises, The Meg, The Beekeeper and Wrath of Man. His last film, Mutiny, was released this year and described by The Hollywood Reporter as “a serviceable maritime action flick that just about stays afloat.”

Jason Statham & Guy Ritchie Reunite For Netflix Feature ‘Dogfight’

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Jason Statham will star in Dogfight, a new Netflix feature written, directed, and produced by Guy Ritchie.

The streamer has said production is slated to begin in early 2027, with filming taking place in the UK. The production companies are Black Bear, Toff Guy Films, and Punch Palace. Producing alongside Ritchie are Statham, Ivan Atkinson, and John Friedberg.

The film is described as a “stylish revenge actioner set in the London criminal underworld.”

Dogfight will be the seventh film collaboration between Ritchie and Statham. Their film credits include Lock, Stock and Two Smoking Barrels, Snatch, and the upcoming film Viva La Madness. Related Stories News Zoe Kazan's 'East Of Eden' Adaptation Manifests Audience Growth Comparable To 'Adolescence' -- Here's How It Happened Acquisitions Netflix Acquires Worldwide On Joel Coen's 'Jack Of Spades' For Fall 2027 Theatrical Release

Viva La Madness is based on the novel of the same name by J.J. Connolly, who co-wrote the screenplay for the film adaptation with Ritchie. Watch on Deadline

Connolly’s debut novel, Layer Cake, was previously adapted for the screen by Matthew Vaughn. Starring in the film alongside Statham are Vinnie Jones (Snatch, Lock, Stock and Two Smoking Barrels), Jason Isaacs (White Lotus, Harry Potter), Babs Olusanmokun (Dune, The Ministry of Ungentlemanly Warfare), Camila Mendes (Riverdale, Palm Springs), Ben Foster (Motor City, Hell or High Water), Jonny Lee Miller (The Crown, Elementary), and Raúl Alejandro, making his feature film debut.

Viva La Madness was developed by Statham for Punch Palace Productions and Thomas Benski for Lumina Studios, who are producing alongside Ritchie and Ivan Atkinson for Toff Guy Films and John Friedberg for Black Bear. Black Bear will distribute the film domestically, while Amazon MGM Studios will distribute it in the majority of international territories.
